# FOOTNOTES AND SCHOLARLY COMMENTARY



[^1]: Printed in Cyrillic numerals: *҂ац҃ѕ* (1000 + 900 + 6 = 1906). The typography of the Stauropegion Institute in Lviv (attached to the historical Dormition Church and Brotherhood founded in the 16th century) published standard liturgical books for the Greek Catholic Church in Galicia. Physical Leaf p1 = Unnumbered Title Page.

[^2]: Inscription dedicates this exemplar to Bishop Ivan Prasko (1914–2001), Greek Catholic Bishop for Ukrainian Catholics in Australia, New Zealand, and Oceania (consecrated 1958, Eparch of SS. Peter and Paul of Melbourne from 1982), from Fr. Ihor Shpytkovsky, dated in Melbourne, July 29, 1968. Physical Leaf p1 = Unnumbered Title Page.

[^3]: Engraving of the architectural ensemble of the Lviv Stauropegion Dormition Church (Успенська церква) on Ruska Street in Lviv, featuring the Korniakt Tower (66 meters, built 1572–1578 by architect Peter Barbon), the Chapel of the Three Hierarchs (built 1578–1591 by Peter Krasowski), and the main church (built 1591–1629 by Paulo Romano). The bottom calligraphic vyaz inscription reads: *ТВОРЕНІЕ ЦАРЕВО И СЫНА ЕГО* ("The Work of the King and His Son"), evoking both the celestial King and His Son and the royal patrons who endowed the brotherhood. Physical Leaf p2 = Full-page frontispiece plate.

[^4]: The official hierarchy dedicatory leaf dated June 26, 1906 (Decree No. 1946), during the pontificate of Pope St. Pius X (reigned 1903–1914) and the reign of Austro-Hungarian Emperor Franz Joseph I (reigned 1848–1916). Servant of God Andrey Sheptytsky served as Metropolitan of Halych and Archbishop of Lviv from 1900 to 1944. Physical Leaf p3 = Dedicatory leaf.

[^5]: Mykola Herasymovych and Roman Ilnytsky served as Senior Wardens (*старѣйшины*) of the Stauropegion Institute. Fr. Volodymyr Hurhula (1866–1938) was a prominent Greek Catholic priest, liturgist, and editor of liturgical books at the Stauropegion typography. Physical Leaf p3 = Dedicatory leaf.

[^6]: An opening prayer to Our Lord Jesus Christ traditionally printed before the liturgical corpus in Ruthenian and Slavic Triodia, beseeching divine grace, compunction, and perseverance through the upcoming holy fast. Physical Leaf p4 = Unnumbered front matter prayer leaf.

[^7]: Physical Leaf p5 = Printed Page 1. Here begins the liturgical corpus of the Lenten Triodion (*Triodion Postnaja*). In this edition, physical leaf coordinates relate to printed book pagination by the formula: Physical Leaf = Printed Page + 4.

[^8]: Psalm 140 (LXX; MT Psalm 141).

[^9]: The Anatolian stichera (*Анатолієвы*) are attributed to Anatolius, Patriarch of Constantinople (449–458) or to an 8th/9th-century hymnographer of the same name.

[^10]: The three solemn penitential troparia (*Покаянія отверзи ми двери, Жизнодавче*, *На спасения стези настави мя, Богородице*, *Множества содѣланныхъ мною лютыхъ*) are chanted at Sunday Matins after Psalm 50 beginning on the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee and continuing through the Fifth Sunday of Great Lent.

[^11]: St. George of Nicomedia (9th century), chartophylax of the Great Church and metropolitan of Nicomedia, prominent Byzantine hymnographer. The Greek acrostic ΓΕΩΡΓΙΟΥ ("Of George") is formed by the initial letters of the Theotokia across the nine odes of the canon and is preserved in Church Slavonic transcription in the printed margins (*Г-Є-Ѡ-Р-Г-І-Ѡ-У*). Physical Leaf p6 = Printed Page 2.

[^12]: Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos (c. 1256–c. 1335), Byzantine ecclesiastical historian, poet, and monk of Hagia Sophia in Constantinople, author of the verse synaxaria and historical introductions for the movable festal cycle of the Triodion and Pentecostarion. Physical Leaf p8 = Printed Page 4.

[^13]: St. Cosmas the Melodist (Bishop of Maiuma, c. 675–752), foster-brother of St. John of Damascus, who first instituted canons consisting of three odes (*triodia*) for the days of Great and Holy Passion Week. The corpus was subsequently expanded by St. Theodore the Studite (759–826) and his brother St. Joseph the Studite (d. 830) to encompass the entire pre-Lenten and Lenten cycle. Physical Leaf p8 = Printed Page 4.

[^14]: The Resurrectional Exapostilarion (or Hymn of Light, *Свѣтиленъ*) of the Octoechos is chanted according to the weekly eothinon (Gospel reading) cycle, followed by the special proper Exapostilarion of the Triodion for the Publican and the Pharisee (*Высокорѣчія убѣжимъ фарисеева злѣйшаго*). Physical Leaf p10 = Printed Page 6.

[^15]: Physical Leaf p11 = Printed Page 7. The 11 Matins Gospel stichera (*Евангельскіѧ стихи̑ры* or *Eothina*) are attributed to Emperor Leo VI the Wise (886–912) and correspond to the 11 resurrectional Gospel pericopes of the Sunday Matins cycle. In monastic and cathedral Typika, from the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee through All Saints Sunday, the designated eothinon sticheron is chanted at the Litiya in the narthex.

[^16]: The Catechetical Homilies (*Ѡ҆глаше́ніѧ*) of St. Theodore the Studite (759–826), Abbot of the Monastery of Studios in Constantinople, provide spiritual instruction on monastic vigilance, ascetic struggle, and compunction. They are appointed to be read to the brethren in the narthex following the Litiya dismissal or at the First Hour throughout the pre-Lenten and Lenten cycle. Physical Leaf p11 = Printed Page 7.

[^17]: 2 Timothy 3:10–15 (Pericope 296). Physical Leaf p11 = Printed Page 7.

[^18]: Luke 18:10–14 (Pericope 84), the Parable of the Publican and the Pharisee. Physical Leaf p11 = Printed Page 7.

[^19]: The "Fast of Artzivurion" (*арцивꙋ́ріевъ по́стъ*, Armenian *Arachavorats*) was a traditional pre-Lenten fast of the Armenian Apostolic Church held ten weeks before Easter (the week following the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee). In Byzantine and Ruthenian polemical rubrics, it was condemned as a Monophysite fast, in response to which the Byzantine Typikon institutes a fast-free week (*сплошная седмица*) permitting cheese, eggs, and dairy for monastics and meat for laity on Wednesday and Friday. Physical Leaf p11 = Printed Page 7.

[^20]: The Resurrectional Theotokion of the Octoechos (Tone 6, by St. John of Damascus): "My Maker and Redeemer, Christ the Lord, having come forth from thy womb, O all-pure one, and having clothed Himself in me, has delivered Adam from the primal curse." Physical Leaf p12 = Printed Page 8.

[^21]: Psalm 136 (LXX; MT Psalm 137). In Ruthenian and Slavic practice, this psalm is solemnly chanted at Sunday Matins after the Polyeleos Psalms (134 and 135) with ornate or festive Alleluia (*со аллилуіею красною*) on three Sundays only: the Sunday of the Prodigal Son, Meatfare Sunday, and Cheesefare Sunday. Physical Leaf p12 = Printed Page 8.

[^22]: Cross-reference to printed folio 2 of the Triodion (Physical Leaf p6), where the penitential post-Gospel troparia (*Покаянія отверзи ми двери, Жизнодавче*, *На спасения стези*, *Множества содѣланныхъ мною лютыхъ*) are printed in full. Physical Leaf p12 = Printed Page 8.

[^23]: St. Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886), monk of Constantinople and one of the preeminent liturgical poets of the Byzantine Church, composer of canons in the Parakletike, Menaia, and Triodion. Physical Leaf p12 = Printed Page 8.

[^24]: Luke 15:18, 21. Physical Leaf p13 = Printed Page 9.

[^25]: Habakkuk 3:3 (LXX). Physical Leaf p13 = Printed Page 9.

[^26]: Model troparion (*podoben*) "Today the Virgin" (*Дѣва днесь*) composed by St. Romanos the Melodist for the Nativity of Christ. Physical Leaf p14 = Printed Page 10.

[^27]: Synaxarion for the Sunday of the Prodigal Son, authored by Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos (c. 1256–c. 1335), monk and ecclesiastical historian of Constantinople. Physical Leaf p14 = Printed Page 10.

[^28]: In the Sunday Praises (*Хвалитехъ*), when 8 stichera are appointed, the Byzantine and Ruthenian order combines 4 Resurrectional stichera of the Octoechos, 1 Anatolian (Eastern) sticheron, and 3 idiomelon stichera of the Triodion. Physical Leaf p16 = Printed Page 12.

[^29]: 1 Corinthians 6:12–20 (Pericope 135). Physical Leaf p16 = Printed Page 12.

[^30]: Luke 15:11–32 (Pericope 79), the Parable of the Prodigal Son. Physical Leaf p16 = Printed Page 12.

[^31]: St. Macarius the Great (c. 300–391), Egyptian desert father. His ascetic homilies were appointed for spiritual reading during pre-Lenten and Lenten Matins. Physical Leaf p16 = Printed Page 12.

[^32]: Physical Leaf p17 = Printed Page 13. Meatfare Saturday is the first Universal Ancestral Saturday of the Dead (*Вселенская родительская суббота*) in the liturgical year, dedicated to the general commemoration of all departed Orthodox Christians from Adam to the present day, celebrated on the eve of the Sunday of the Last Judgment.

[^33]: One of the classic funeral idiomela (*самогласны*) composed by St. John of Damascus (c. 676–749), which also forms an integral part of the Byzantine burial office (*Otpevanie*). Physical Leaf p17 = Printed Page 13.

[^34]: Psalm 64:5 (LXX; MT Psalm 65:4) and Psalm 101:13 (LXX; MT Psalm 102:12). Physical Leaf p17 = Printed Page 13.

[^35]: Psalm 24:13 (LXX; MT Psalm 25:13). Physical Leaf p17 = Printed Page 13.

[^36]: St. John of Damascus (c. 676–749), Doctor of the Church and monk of the Laura of St. Sabbas the Sanctified, author of the Funeral Stichera of the Octoechos. Physical Leaf p17 = Printed Page 13.

[^37]: The Panikhida (*Паннихида*, from Greek παννυχίς, "all-night service") is the Byzantine memorial office for the departed, served in the narthex following Vespers on memorial days. Physical Leaf p18 = Printed Page 14.

[^38]: 1 Corinthians 15:12–58, St. Paul's exposition of the resurrection of the dead. Physical Leaf p18 = Printed Page 14.

[^39]: Psalm 118 (LXX; MT Psalm 119), known as the *Amomos* (from Greek Ἄμωμοι, "Blameless"), is divided into two sections (*staseis*) at funeral Matins, with distinct refrains. Physical Leaf p18 = Printed Page 14.

[^40]: Psalm 118:12 (LXX; MT Psalm 119:12), the refrain to the Funeral Evlogitaria (*Непорочны*), Tone 5. Physical Leaf p18 = Printed Page 14.

[^41]: The solemn priestly prayer for the deceased: "O God of spirits and of all flesh, Who hast trampled down death and overthrown the Devil..." from the Byzantine Euchologion (*Sluzhebnik* / *Trebnik*). Physical Leaf p18 = Printed Page 14.

[^42]: St. Theodore the Studite (759–826). His Canon for Meatfare Saturday systematically catalogues all conceivable manners of human demise (drowning, war, earthquakes, wild beasts, pestilence, fire, cold) so that no departed Christian remains unremembered by the church. Physical Leaf p19 = Printed Page 15.

[^43]: The Slavonic rubric translates the acrostic as "Недосто́йнагѡ Ѳео́дѡра" ("Of unworthy Theodore"), but the printed marginal letters transliterate the original Byzantine Greek acrostic composed by St. Theodore the Studite: ΤΟΥ ΤΑΠΕΙΝΟΥ ΘΕΟΔΩΡΟΥ ("Of the humble Theodore"). The letters printed in the margins across Odes 1–4 are: Ode 1: Т, О; Ode 2: Y, Т; Ode 3: А, П; Ode 4: Е, І, spelling ΤΟΥ ΤΑΠΕΙ... Physical Leaf p19 = Printed Page 15.

[^44]: Church Slavonic: *плинда*, a loanword from Byzantine Greek πλίνθος ("brick", "masonry tile"). Physical Leaf p20 = Printed Page 16.

[^45]: Typographical error in the 1906 Stauropegion edition, which prints *Г҃дороди́тельнице* instead of *Бг҃ороди́тельнице*. Physical Leaf p20 = Printed Page 16.

[^46]: Physical Leaf p21 = Printed Page 17. The Greek acrostic in the margins: Ode 5 has Ѳ (Theta) at Glory and V (Upsilon) at Now and ever; Ode 6 has О (Omicron) at Glory and Д (Delta) at Now and ever, continuing the Greek acrostic ΤΟΥ ТАПЕΙΝΟΥ ΘΕΟΔΩΡΟΥ ("Of the humble Theodore") composed by St. Theodore the Studite (759–826).

[^47]: Synaxarion for Meatfare Saturday, authored by Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos (c. 1256–c. 1335), monk and ecclesiastical historian of Constantinople. It expounds the theological rationale for commemorating all departed Orthodox Christians on the eve of the Sunday of the Dread Judgment. Physical Leaf p21 = Printed Page 17.

[^48]: Genesis 3:19, quoted in the Ikos: "For dust thou art, and unto dust shalt thou return." Physical Leaf p21 = Printed Page 17.

[^49]: Dionysius the Areopagite (*Ecclesiastical Hierarchy*, VII.3), St. Gregory the Theologian (*Funeral Oration on his Brother Caesarius*, Or. 7), and St. John Chrysostom (*Homilies on Philippians*, Homily 3:4–5), establishing the apostolic foundation of prayers, divine liturgies, and almsgiving on behalf of the deceased. Physical Leaf p22 = Printed Page 18.

[^50]: Physical Leaf p22 = Printed Page 18. Margins of Ode 7 display acrostic letters Ѡ (Omega) at Glory and Р (Rho) at Now and ever, continuing St. Theodore the Studite's acrostic.

[^51]: The catalogue of unusual causes of death in Ode 8 reflects St. Theodore the Studite's pastoral care to include every soul who suffered sudden, violent, or accidental demise without the church's final prayers. Physical Leaf p22 = Printed Page 18.

[^52]: Physical Leaf p23 = Printed Page 19. Margins of Ode 8 show acrostic letters О and V, and Ode 9 repeats О and V to finalize the acrostic of St. Theodore the Studite.

[^53]: The Canticle of the Theotokos (*Magnificat* / *Честнѣйшую*) is omitted on Meatfare Saturday at Ode 9, proceeding immediately to the Heirmos and Katavasia of Ode 9 of the Triodion canon. Physical Leaf p23 = Printed Page 19.

[^54]: Song of Songs 4:12 ("A garden enclosed is my sister, my spouse; a spring shut up, a fountain sealed"), applied typologically to the perpetual virginity of the Theotokos in Ode 8 Theotokion. Physical Leaf p23 = Printed Page 19.

[^55]: Physical Leaf p24 = Printed Page 20. The printed Slavonic heading displays *гла́съ к҃. Ме́ртвенъ* (Tone 20), an obvious typographical erratum in the 1906 Stauropegion edition for *гла́съ в҃* (Tone 2).

[^56]: Psalm 64:5 (LXX; MT Psalm 65:4) quoted in the Theotokion: "Holy is Thy temple, wonderful in righteousness." Physical Leaf p24 = Printed Page 20.

[^57]: At the Aposticha, the prosomoia appointed from the Octoechos for the dead are attributed to St. Theophanes the Branded (c. 775–845), Metropolitan of Nicaea, followed by the idiomelon of St. John of Damascus. Physical Leaf p24 = Printed Page 20.

[^58]: Liturgical scriptural pericopes for Meatfare Saturday Divine Liturgy: Epistle: 1 Corinthians 10:23–28 (Pericope 141) and 1 Thessalonians 4:13–17 (Pericope 270, for the departed); Gospel: Luke 21:8–9, 25–27, 33–36 (Pericope 105) and John 5:24–30 (Pericope 16, for the departed). Prokeimenon: Psalm 24:13 (LXX; MT Psalm 25:13); Alleluia: Psalm 64:5 (LXX; MT Psalm 65:4); Communion Hymn (*Koinonikon*): Psalm 64:5. Physical Leaf p24 = Printed Page 20.

[^59]: This entire order (*poslidovanie*) of Ancestral Saturday is prescribed to be served identically on Saturday before Pentecost (Trinity Ancestral Saturday / *Troyitska rodytelska subota*). Physical Leaf p24 = Printed Page 20.

[^60]: Physical Leaf p25 = Printed Page 21. Headpiece: Woodcut depiction of the Dread Judgment with Christ enthroned as Pantokrator and Judge of all creation. Meatfare Sunday (or the Sunday of the Dread Judgment) is the penultimate Sunday of the pre-Lenten preparatory season, after which meat is forbidden for the remainder of the fast.

[^61]: Daniel 7:9–10 ("The thrones were set up... A fiery stream issued and came forth from before Him... the judgment was set, and the books were opened"). Physical Leaf p25 = Printed Page 21.

[^62]: Matthew 25:31–46, the Gospel of the Dread Judgment read at the Divine Liturgy of Meatfare Sunday. Physical Leaf p25 = Printed Page 21.

[^63]: Matthew 25:34. Physical Leaf p26 = Printed Page 22.

[^64]: St. Gregory the Theologian's *Oration 14: On Love for the Poor* (*De pauperum amore* / *Слово о нищелюбии*), beginning: "Men and brethren, and fellow-poor..." is appointed to be read in three sections during Matins. Physical Leaf p26 = Printed Page 22.

[^65]: Cross-reference to printed folio 2 of the Triodion (Physical Leaf p6), where the penitential post-Gospel stichera are printed in full. Physical Leaf p26 = Printed Page 22.

[^66]: The Canon of Meatfare Sunday is the composition of St. Theodore the Studite (759–826). The Heirmos of Ode 1 is taken from the Great Canon of St. Andrew of Crete (Exodus 15:1–2). Physical Leaf p26 = Printed Page 22.

[^67]: 1 Thessalonians 4:17 ("Then we who are alive and remain shall be caught up together with them in the clouds to meet the Lord in the air"). Physical Leaf p26 = Printed Page 22.

[^68]: Physical Leaf p27 = Printed Page 23. The Triadicon troparion opens Ode 1's concluding Trinitarian doxology.

[^69]: Joel 2:11 / Malachi 3:2 ("For the day of the Lord is great and very terrible; who can endure it?"). Physical Leaf p27 = Printed Page 23.

[^70]: Matthew 25:10 ("And the door was shut"). Physical Leaf p27 = Printed Page 23.

[^71]: Psalm 83:7 (LXX; MT Psalm 84:6), the "valley of weeping" (*юдоль плача* / Hebrew *Emek HaBakha*). Physical Leaf p27 = Printed Page 23.

[^72]: The printed Slavonic has the curious compound/corrupt form *бꙋдовов̾несогла́тєльныѧ* (representing Greek ἀκαтаλλάκτους, "implacable" / "irreconcilable", or representing people in dire distress and unrelenting trial). Physical Leaf p27 = Printed Page 23.

[^73]: Matthew 22:11–13, the parable of the wedding garment: "Bind him hand and foot, and take him away, and cast him into outer darkness." Physical Leaf p27 = Printed Page 23.

[^74]: Physical Leaf p28 = Printed Page 24. Matthew 25:32–33, the separation of the sheep from the goats.

[^75]: Luke 16:19–31, the Parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus. Physical Leaf p28 = Printed Page 24.

[^76]: The abbreviation *Дв҃а* in the Theotokion represents *Дѣ́ва* ("Virgin"), not *два* ("two"), affirming that the Virgin, having given birth to an Infant, remained pure. Physical Leaf p28 = Printed Page 24.

[^77]: Ezekiel 44:2 ("This gate shall be shut, it shall not be opened, and no man shall enter in by it; because the Lord, the God of Israel, hath entered in by it, therefore it shall be shut"), interpreted typologically of the Virgin's perpetual virginity. Physical Leaf p29 = Printed Page 25.

[^78]: Synaxarion of Meatfare Sunday (of the Second and Incorrupt Coming of Christ), authored by Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos (c. 1256–c. 1335). It contextualizes the Dread Judgment following the two parables (Publican and Pharisee, Prodigal Son) to prevent laxity and false confidence in mercy without repentance. Physical Leaf p29 = Printed Page 25.

[^79]: St. Hippolytus of Rome (c. 170–235), *Treatise on Christ and Antichrist* (*De Christo et Antichristo*), and St. John of Damascus (*Exact Exposition of the Orthodox Faith*, IV.26), detailing the eschatological deception, the tribe of Dan (Genesis 49:17), the 2 Thessalonians 2:3–10 prophecy, and the preaching of Enoch and Elijah (Revelation 11:3–12). Physical Leaf p29 = Printed Page 25.

[^80]: Physical Leaf p30 = Printed Page 26. The transfiguration and renewal of creation at the general resurrection (Romans 8:19–22).

[^81]: Matthew 25:35–40. The six works of bodily mercy: feeding the hungry, giving drink to the thirsty, sheltering the stranger, clothing the naked, visiting the sick, visiting the imprisoned. Physical Leaf p30 = Printed Page 26.

[^82]: Philippians 2:11 ("And that every tongue should confess that Jesus Christ is Lord, to the glory of God the Father"). Physical Leaf p30 = Printed Page 26.

[^83]: Mark 9:48 ("Where their worm dies not, and the fire is not quenched") and Matthew 22:13 / 25:30 ("Cast ye the unprofitable servant into outer darkness: there shall be weeping and gnashing of teeth"). Physical Leaf p30 = Printed Page 26.

[^84]: Psalm 50:3 (LXX; MT Psalm 51:1) invoked by King David: "Have mercy on me, O God, according to Thy great mercy." Physical Leaf p30 = Printed Page 26.

[^85]: Daniel 3:29–31 / Prayer of the Three Holy Children 1:5–8 ("For we have sinned, and committed iniquity..."). Physical Leaf p30 = Printed Page 26.

[^86]: Physical Leaf p31 = Printed Page 27. On the Triadicon distinction "in personal definition other, but not differing in nature" (*ѡ҆предѣли́тельнѣ и҆́ныѧ, но не и҆на́кѡвныѧ* / Greek *ὑποστατικῶς ἄλλας, ἀλλ' οὐκ ἀλλοίας*), affirming classical Cappadocian trinitarian theology distinguishing distinct Hypostases (*allos kai allos*) from identical divine essence (*heteroousios*).

[^87]: Exodus 33:23 ("And I will take away mine hand, and thou shalt see my back parts: but my face shall not be seen"). Quoted in Ode 9, comparing Moses' awe at beholding God from behind with the sinner's dread before the face of Christ at His Second Coming. Physical Leaf p31 = Printed Page 27.

[^88]: Daniel 7:9–10, the dread vision of the Ancient of Days seated upon the throne and the river of fire issuing forth. Physical Leaf p31 = Printed Page 27.

[^89]: Physical Leaf p32 = Printed Page 28. "And all shall be in one stature" (*и҆ во́зрастомъ є҆ди́нѣмъ всѝ бꙋ́дꙋтъ*), reflecting patristic eschatology (cf. Ephesians 4:13, "unto the measure of the stature of the fullness of Christ," traditionally interpreted as the resurrection of all at the perfect age of thirty-three).

[^90]: Daniel 9:23; 10:11, 19, where Daniel is addressed by the Archangel Gabriel as a "man of desires" (*мꙋ́жъ жела́нїй* / Vulgate *vir desideriorum* / Greek *ἀνὴρ ἐπιθυμιῶν*), indicating one beloved of God and yearning after divine righteousness. Physical Leaf p32 = Printed Page 28.

[^91]: Physical Leaf p33 = Printed Page 29. The Catechesis (*Ѻ҆глаше́нїе*) of St. Theodore the Studite (759–826), Abbot of the Studion Monastery in Constantinople, appointed to be read at the First Hour during the fasting cycles of the liturgical year.

[^92]: The spiritual understanding of the Great Fast as the "tithes of the whole year" (*десѧти́ны всегѡ̀ лѣ́та*): offering 36.5 days (one tenth of the 365 days of the solar year) unto God in dedicated fasting and repentance (cf. St. Dorotheus of Gaza, *Discourses*, 15; St. John Cassian, *Conferences*, 21). Physical Leaf p33 = Printed Page 29.

[^93]: Physical Leaf p34 = Printed Page 30. St. Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886), monk of the Studion Monastery, who composed the triodia (three-ode canons) for the weekdays of Cheesefare Week and Great Lent.

[^94]: Cheesefare Week is designated as the "all-honorable pre-purifying week" (*предочисти́тельнаѧ седми́ца всечтна́ѧ*) of the holy fasts, transitioning the faithful from the full consumption of meat to the strict fast of Great Lent through the intermediate abstinence from meat while permitting dairy and eggs. Physical Leaf p34 = Printed Page 30.

[^95]: Ezekiel 44:2 ("This gate shall be shut, it shall not be opened, and no man shall enter in by it; because the Lord, the God of Israel, hath entered in by it, therefore it shall be shut"), interpreted typologically by the Church Fathers as an image of the perpetual virginity of the Theotokos. Physical Leaf p35 = Printed Page 31.

[^96]: The patristic teaching that fasting purifies the soul and bodily senses, drawing the guardian angels to abide more closely with the Christian (cf. St. Basil the Great, *Homily 1 on Fasting*, 7). Physical Leaf p35 = Printed Page 31.

[^97]: Physical Leaf p36 = Printed Page 32. Dietary rubric for Cheesefare Week: "Be it known that on Monday of Cheesefare Week we eat twice in the day" (*въ понедѣ́льникъ сы́рныѧ недѣ́ли ꙗ҆ди́мъ два́жды дне́мъ*). Unlike Clean Week when only a single evening xerophagic meal is taken, during Cheesefare Week two meals containing dairy products, eggs, and fish are permitted on non-fasting weekdays.

[^98]: St. Basil the Great (330–379), *Homily on Thanksgiving* (*De gratiarum actione* / *сло́во ѡ҆ бл҃годарє́ніи*, PG 31:217–237), expounding 1 Thessalonians 5:18 ("In everything give thanks"). Physical Leaf p36 = Printed Page 32.

[^99]: Proverbs 19:17 ("He that hath pity upon the poor lends unto the Lord; and that which he hath given will He pay him again"), applied to Christ in Matthew 25:40 ("Inasmuch as ye have done it unto one of the least of these my brethren, ye have done it unto Me"). Physical Leaf p37 = Printed Page 33.

[^100]: Exodus 3:2, the burning bush on Mount Sinai that burned with fire and was not consumed, the preeminent Old Testament type of the Theotokos bearing the divine fire of the Godhead without being burned. Physical Leaf p37 = Printed Page 33.

[^101]: Jonah 3:5–10 (the repentance of Nineveh at the preaching of Jonah) and Luke 7:37–50; 18:9–14; 19:1–10 (the repentance of publicans and harlots), cited as paradigms of true repentance at the threshold of Great Lent. Physical Leaf p38 = Printed Page 34.

[^102]: Physical Leaf p38 = Printed Page 34. Rubric indicating the start of the penitential lenten discipline: from Cheesefare Tuesday evening Vespers, full metanias (earthly prostrations) begin, accompanied by the Prayer of St. Ephrem the Syrian (*Lord and Master of my life*), with 16 prostrations (4 great, 12 small with the prayer *O God, cleanse me a sinner*, then 1 great).

[^103]: The liturgical rule for Wednesday and Friday of Cheesefare Week appoints the full Lenten order (Alleluia instead of *God is the Lord*, Lenten kathismata, prostrations at all services, and strict fasting without Liturgy), but omits the Inter-Hours (*Междоча́сїа же не пое́мъ*), which are chanted only during the forty days of Great Lent proper. Physical Leaf p39 = Printed Page 35.

[^104]: St. Basil the Great, *First Homily on Fasting* (*De jejunio 1* / *сло́во ѡ҆ постѣ̀*, PG 31:163–184), traditionally appointed to be read at Matins on Cheesefare Wednesday. Physical Leaf p39 = Printed Page 35.

[^105]: St. Andrew of Crete (c. 660–740), Archbishop of Gortyna and author of the Great Penitential Canon, who composed the four-ode canon for Cheesefare Wednesday Matins. Physical Leaf p39 = Printed Page 35.

[^106]: Genesis 5:24 ("And Enoch walked with God: and he was not; for God took him") and Hebrews 11:5, cited typologically as one whose ascetic bodily purity and abstinence merited translation without seeing death. Physical Leaf p40 = Printed Page 36.

[^107]: Matthew 4:1–11 and Luke 4:1–13. Christ fasted forty days in the wilderness in His human nature to conquer the devil, establishing the paradigm, pattern, and boundary for Christian ascetical fasting. Physical Leaf p40 = Printed Page 36.

[^108]: Genesis 18:1–16. Abraham receiving the three angelic visitors under the Oak of Mamre, recognized patristically as an Old Testament typological manifestation (*theophany*) of the Holy Trinity. Physical Leaf p41 = Printed Page 37.

[^109]: Exodus 24:18; 34:28 (Moses fasting forty days on Mount Sinai to receive the Law and behold God) and 4 Kingdoms 2:11 (Elijah, having fasted forty days in 3 Kingdoms 19:8, taken up to heaven in a fiery chariot). Physical Leaf p41 = Printed Page 37.

[^110]: Isaiah 6:6–7. The Seraph touching the prophet's lips with a live coal taken with tongs from the altar, purging his sin; presented here as an image of the purifying power of ascetic fasting. Physical Leaf p41 = Printed Page 37.

[^111]: Daniel 6:16–23 (Daniel preserved unharmed from the lions in the den through his fasting and prayers) and Daniel 3:19–28 (the Three Holy Youths treading the flames of the furnace in Babylon). Physical Leaf p41 = Printed Page 37.

[^112]: Jonah 2:1–10 (Jonah delivered from the sea monster) and Jonah 3:5–10 (the citizens of Nineveh turning aside the wrath of God through urgent fasting and repentance). Physical Leaf p41 = Printed Page 37.

[^113]: Genesis 39:7–12 (Joseph preserving his chastity by fleeing the wife of Potiphar, subsequently exalted to rule Egypt) and 1 Kingdoms 17:40–51 (David conquering the Philistine Goliath through ascetic reliance upon God). Physical Leaf p42 = Printed Page 38.

[^114]: Exodus 17:11–12. Moses stretching out his arms in the form of a cross on the mountain, enabling Israel to conquer Amalek; understood typologically as Christ outstretching His hands upon the Cross to slay the tyranny of hades. Physical Leaf p42 = Printed Page 38.

[^115]: Luke 23:39–43. The Good Thief confessing Christ upon the Cross amidst the solar eclipse at the crucifixion (Matthew 27:45), thereby being granted entrance into Paradise. Physical Leaf p42 = Printed Page 38.

[^116]: The theological confession of the consubstantial Trinity (*є҆диносꙋ́щнаѧ Тро́йца*) contra the heresy of Arius (*А҆́рїево ᲂу҆че́нїе*), which denied the co-eternal divinity of the Son; condemned at the First Ecumenical Council of Nicaea (325 AD). Physical Leaf p42 = Printed Page 38.

[^117]: Exodus 14:21–22 (Moses parting the Red Sea) and Joshua 3:14–17; 4:1–18 (Joshua sanctifying Israel and leading them across the Jordan into the Promised Land). Physical Leaf p43 = Printed Page 39.

[^118]: Judges 7:4–8. Gideon selecting the three hundred men who lapped water rather than kneeling, triumphing over the Midianite army through ascetic discipline and prayer. Physical Leaf p43 = Printed Page 39.

[^119]: Romans 8:35 ("Who shall separate us from the love of Christ? Shall tribulation, or distress, or persecution, or famine, or nakedness, or peril, or sword?"), cited in the Martyrion of Cheesefare Wednesday Matins. Physical Leaf p44 = Printed Page 40.

[^120]: The penitential liturgical discipline of Great Lent instituted on Cheesefare Wednesday and Friday: reciting the Prayer of St. Ephrem the Syrian (*Lord and Master of my life*) with 16 prostrations (4 great, 12 small with the prayer *O God, cleanse me a sinner*, followed by 1 great). Physical Leaf p44 = Printed Page 40.

[^121]: Joel 2:12–26 (LXX), the prophetic reading appointed for the Sixth Hour on Cheesefare Wednesday, proclaiming the solemn fast and the eschatological restoration of Israel. Physical Leaf p45 = Printed Page 41.

[^122]: Joel 3:12–21 (LXX Joel 4:12–21), the prophetic reading appointed for Cheesefare Wednesday Vespers, depicting the universal judgment of the nations in the Valley of Jehoshaphat (*ю҆до́ль І҆ѡсафа́товꙋ*). Physical Leaf p46 = Printed Page 42.

[^123]: The Palestinian Studite Typikon rubric: neither the full Divine Liturgy nor the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts is celebrated on Wednesday and Friday of Cheesefare Week; instead, the services follow the penitential Lenten order with *Alleluia* and prostrations. Physical Leaf p47 = Printed Page 43.

[^124]: Patriarch St. Nicephorus the Confessor of Constantinople (c. 758–828), defending the Orthodox rule of fasting on Cheesefare Wednesday and Friday until evening while permitting dairy and eggs at supper, to reject both the Jacobite Monophysite total fast and the Tetradite heresy. Physical Leaf p47 = Printed Page 43.

[^125]: St. Anastasius of Sinai (7th c.), Abbot of St. Catherine's Monastery, whose ascetic homilies are appointed for reading at Matins during Cheesefare Week. Physical Leaf p47 = Printed Page 43.

[^126]: The Prayer of Manasseh (2 Chronicles 33:11–13 / LXX Odes 12), the preeminent biblical model of sincere repentance: "I have sinned more than the sands of the sea." Physical Leaf p48 = Printed Page 44.

[^127]: Genesis 4:3–5. The unacceptable offering of Cain, who offered sacrifice with an impure and unrepentant mind, contrasted with the acceptable spiritual sacrifice of the fast. Physical Leaf p48 = Printed Page 44.

[^128]: Galatians 5:24 ("And they that are Christ's have crucified the flesh with the affections and lusts"), paraphrased in the Aposticha idiomelon of Cheesefare Thursday Vespers. Physical Leaf p50 = Printed Page 46.

[^129]: Cross-reference to fol. 35 (Physical Leaf p39 = Printed Page 35): "Compline we chant Great with prostrations, as was indicated for Tuesday: fol. 35" (*ли́стъ л҃є*).

[^130]: St. Basil the Great, *Second Homily on Fasting* (*De jejunio 2* / *сло́во ѡ҆ постѣ̀ в҃*, PG 31:185–197), opening with the citation of Psalm 80:4 (LXX): "Blow the trumpet at the new moon" (*Вострꙋби́те въ новомѣ́сѧчїи трꙋбо́ю*), appointed to be read at Cheesefare Friday Matins. Physical Leaf p50 = Printed Page 46.

[^131]: Matthew 25:1–13. The Parable of the Ten Virgins, the patristic and liturgical archetype of ascetic vigilance, whose trimmed lamps filled with the oil of merciful deeds permit entrance into the heavenly bridal chamber (*свѣ́тлый черто́гъ*) of Christ the Bridegroom. Physical Leaf p51 = Printed Page 47.

[^132]: Colossians 2:14. The canceling and tearing up of the record of debt (*рꙋкописа́нїе / χειρόγραφον*) that stood against fallen humanity, nailed by Christ unto His Cross; invoked here through the maternal intercession of the Theotokos. Physical Leaf p51 = Printed Page 47.

[^133]: Exodus 24:18; 34:28. Moses ascending Mount Sinai and fasting forty days and forty nights without bread or water, thereby being accounted worthy to behold the glory of God and receive the tablets of the Law; presented as the preeminent Old Testament model for the ascetic ladder of divine ascent (*бж҃е́ственнаѧ восхожде́нїа*). Physical Leaf p51 = Printed Page 47.

[^134]: Colossians 2:14 and the ancient patristic motif of Adam's bond of indebtedness (*писа́нїе па́дшагѡ А҆да́ма*), which according to liturgical tradition the deceitful serpent had extracted from fallen Adam, torn asunder by Christ when His hands were nailed and His side pierced on Golgotha. Physical Leaf p52 = Printed Page 48.

[^135]: The classic Cappadocian and Damascene Trinitarian formulation: the Father does not depart from His unbegotten hypostasis into Sonship (*ни Ѻ҆ц҃ꙋ̀ и҆зстꙋ́пльшꙋ въ сыновство̀*), nor does the Son exchange His begotten hypostasis for the procession of the Spirit (*нижѐ Сн҃ꙋ премѣ́никшꙋсѧ во и҆схожде́нїе*); the Persons are distinct yet consubstantial and undivided in the one divine essence. Physical Leaf p52 = Printed Page 48.

[^136]: 4 Kingdoms 2:11 (2 Kings 2:11 LXX). The Prophet Elijah, having fasted forty days in the wilderness (3 Kingdoms 19:8), taken up bodily into heaven upon a chariot of fire; commemorated as the prototype of ascetic mastery over bodily passions. Physical Leaf p53 = Printed Page 49.

[^137]: Daniel 3:19–28. Ananias, Azarias, and Misael preserved unharmed within the Babylonian fiery furnace through their fasting and prayer, treading the flames as if in moist dew (*на ро́сꙋ ѻ҆́гнь преложи́ша*). Physical Leaf p53 = Printed Page 49.

[^138]: Ephesians 2:14. Christ breaking down the barrier and the middle wall of partition (*граде́жа средостѣ́нїе / τὸ μεσότοιχον τοῦ φραγμοῦ*), reconciling humanity unto God through His Cross and establishing universal peace. Physical Leaf p54 = Printed Page 50.

[^139]: Genesis 3:1–19 (Adam's irrational transgression of the primordial fast in Eden) and Colossians 2:14 (Christ nailing the bond of sin unto the wood of the Cross). Physical Leaf p54 = Printed Page 50.

[^140]: Galatians 3:13 ("Christ hath redeemed us from the curse of the law, being made a curse for us: for it is written, Cursed is every one that hangs on a tree"), fulfilling the redemption from the primordial ancestral curse. Physical Leaf p55 = Printed Page 51.

[^141]: Genesis 2:21–22 (the fashioning of Eve from Adam's rib, through which transgression entered the world) and John 19:34 (the piercing of Christ's side on the Cross, from which water and blood flowed forth to heal the corruption that sprouted from the primordial rib). Physical Leaf p55 = Printed Page 51.

[^142]: The penitential Lenten prayer of St. Ephrem the Syrian (*Гдⷭ҇и и҆ Влⷪ҇ко живота̀ моегѡ̀ / Lord and Master of my life*), accompanied by the 16 prostrations (4 great metanias, 12 small with the prayer *O God, cleanse me a sinner*, followed by 1 great metania), concluding the weekday services of Cheesefare Week. Physical Leaf p56 = Printed Page 52.

[^143]: St. Theodore the Studite (759–826), Abbot of the Studion Monastery in Constantinople. His ascetic catechetical homilies (*ѻ҆глаше́нїа* / *Catecheses Magnae et Parvae*) are prescribed in the Byzantine and Studite Typika to be read to the monastic brotherhood at the conclusion of the First Hour throughout Great Lent. Physical Leaf p56 = Printed Page 52.

[^144]: Zechariah 8:7–17 (LXX). The prophetic reading appointed for the Sixth Hour on Cheesefare Friday, promising the divine ingathering of the scattered remnant of Israel from east and west to dwell in Jerusalem, requiring righteousness, truth, and peace. Physical Leaf p57 = Printed Page 53.

[^145]: The traditional Byzantine Typikon rubric: the commemoration of the saints occurring in the Menaion on Cheesefare Saturday and Cheesefare (Forgiveness) Sunday is omitted from the main liturgical cycle (since Saturday is dedicated entirely to All Holy Ascetic Fathers and Sunday to the Expulsion of Adam), and transferred instead to Compline. Physical Leaf p57 = Printed Page 53.

[^146]: The universal liturgical commemoration of All Holy Monastics and Ascetic Fathers and Mothers (*Па́мѧть всѣ́хъ преподо́бныхъ и҆ бг҃оно́сныхъ Ѻ҆тє́цъ, въ по́двиѕѣ просїѧ́вшихъ*), instituted on the Saturday of Cheesefare Week as a spiritual fortification and pattern of ascetic virtue immediately prior to entering Clean Week and the Great Fast. Physical Leaf p58 = Printed Page 54.

[^147]: St. Anthony the Great (c. 251–356), the father and pioneer of Christian eremitic monasticism in the Egyptian desert, and St. Euthymius the Great (377–473), the great organizer and archimandrite of Judean coenobitic and lavra monasticism. Physical Leaf p58 = Printed Page 54.

[^148]: The foundational monastic regions of early Christendom: Lower Egypt (Nitria and Kellia), Upper Egypt (the Thebaid, founded by St. Pachomius), and Libya/Pentapolis, which nurtured the first generations of desert fathers. Physical Leaf p58 = Printed Page 54.

[^149]: 1 Corinthians 13:12 ("For now we see through a glass, darkly [δι' ἐσόπτρου ἐν αἰνίγματι / въ зерца́лѣ въ гада́нїи]; but then face to face"). In heaven, the earthly mirrors of imperfect vision are dissolved (*зерца́ломъ разрѣши́вшымсѧ*), allowing the holy ascetic fathers to behold the Holy Trinity directly and without mediation (*непосре́дственнѡ*). Physical Leaf p59 = Printed Page 55.

[^150]: Zechariah 8:19–23 (LXX). The prophetic reading appointed for Cheesefare Friday Vespers, prophesying that the fasts of the fourth, fifth, seventh, and tenth months shall become joy, gladness, and cheerful feasts to the house of Judah, and that ten men from all nations shall take hold of the robe of a Jew, saying: "We will go with you, for we have heard that God is with you." Physical Leaf p59 = Printed Page 55.

[^151]: 2 Corinthians 7:1 ("let us cleanse ourselves from all filthiness of the flesh and spirit"), Matthew 25:1–13 (entering with Christ and the wise virgins into the bridal chamber), and Luke 23:42 (the confession and supplication of the Good Thief on the Cross: "Lord, remember me when Thou comest into Thy kingdom"). Physical Leaf p59 = Printed Page 55.

[^152]: The *uspokoitelnytsia* (*ᲂу҆спокои́тельницѧ* / Greek: *κοιμητήριον* / Ukrainian: *усипальниця*), the mortuary chapel or charnel house within a monastery, where the Canon for the Departed (*канѡ́нъ ме́ртвенъ*) from the Octoechos is chanted at Compline on Friday evenings preceding Saturday commemorations. Physical Leaf p60 = Printed Page 56.

[^153]: St. Paul of Thebes (c. 227–341), venerated as the first Christian hermit (*Нача́льникъ мона́шествꙋющихъ*), who lived in solitary asceticism in the Egyptian desert for over nine decades and was visited before his repose by St. Anthony the Great; commemorated alongside Anthony and Euthymius at Cheesefare Saturday Matins. Physical Leaf p60 = Printed Page 56.

[^154]: The universal liturgical commemoration of All Holy Monastics and Ascetic Fathers and Mothers (*Па́мѧть всѣ́хъ преподо́бныхъ и҆ бг҃оно́сныхъ Ѻ҆тє́цъ, въ по́двиѕѣ просїѧ́вшихъ*), instituted on the Saturday of Cheesefare Week as a spiritual fortification and pattern of ascetic virtue immediately prior to entering Clean Week and the Great Fast. The troparion invokes the three foundational regions of early desert monasticism: Lower Egypt (Nitria and Kellia), Upper Egypt (the Thebaid, founded by St. Pachomius), and Libya/Pentapolis. Physical Leaf p61 = Printed Page 57.

[^155]: The primary pioneers of Christian monasticism: St. Anthony the Great (c. 251–356), the father of eremitic desert monasticism; St. Ammon of Nitria (c. 294–357), founder of the Nitrian monastic settlements; St. Arsenius the Great (c. 350–445), imperial tutor turned solitary hesychast (*держа́во безмо́лвїѧ*); and St. Pachomius the Great (c. 292–348), founder of Christian coenobitic (communal) monasticism in Tabennisi. Physical Leaf p61 = Printed Page 57.

[^156]: Celebrated Desert Fathers commemorated in the *Apophthegmata Patrum* (*Sayings of the Desert Fathers*): Abba Agathon, Abba Achillas, Abba Ammon, Abba Poemen the Shepherd (c. 340–450), Abba Paësius (brother of Poemen), Abba Pambo of Nitria (d. c. 375), and Abba Isaiah of Scetis (d. 491). Physical Leaf p61 = Printed Page 57.

[^157]: St. Athanasius of Mount Athos (c. 920–1004), founder of the Great Lavra and organized monastic life on the Holy Mountain of Athos, or St. Athanasius the Great (c. 296–373) who found refuge among the desert fathers of Egypt; here associated with ascetic fasting on Mount Idion. Physical Leaf p61 = Printed Page 57.

[^158]: St. Hilarion the Great (291–371), disciple of Anthony who established monasticism in Palestine and Gaza, living a detached, "bird-like" heavenly life (*пти́чее житїѐ*); St. Benjamin of Nitria; St. Helladius; St. Eulogius the Presbyter; and St. Bacchyllus. Physical Leaf p62 = Printed Page 58.

[^159]: Western and Eastern ascetic founders: St. Benedict of Nursia (c. 480–548), the father of Western coenobitic monasticism and author of the Holy Rule; St. John Cassian (c. 360–435), abbot of Saint-Victor in Marseilles and bridge between Egyptian desert wisdom and the West through his *Institutes* and *Conferences*; and St. Bassian the Recluse, who enclosed himself alive in a tomb-like cell (*во гро́бѣ бо живы́й всели́всѧ*) as an emblem of perfect ascetic obedience. Physical Leaf p62 = Printed Page 58.

[^160]: St. Gelasius of Palestine (5th c.), abbot of Nicopolis; St. Gerasimus of the Jordan (d. 475), founder of the famous Judean lavra near Jericho, renowned in hagio-liturgical tradition for his friendship with a wild lion that served him obediently in virtue (*є҆мꙋ́же порабо́та ѕвѣ́рь*); and Father Germanus and Gaius, ascetics of the Egyptian desert. Physical Leaf p62 = Printed Page 58.

[^161]: St. David of Thessalonica (c. 450–535), who spent three years living in an almond tree outside the city walls as a dendrite before dwelling in a small hut; St. Daniel of Scetis (6th c.), archimandrite of the Skete; St. Dius the Wonderworker (d. c. 430), founder of a major monastery in Constantinople; and St. Dalmatus (d. 440), former soldier who became archimandrite of the Dalmation monastery and a valiant defender of Orthodoxy at the Council of Ephesus (431). Physical Leaf p62 = Printed Page 58.

[^162]: St. Euthymius the Great (377–473), founding archimandrite of Judean lavra monasticism; St. Thalassius of Libya (7th c.), hesychast and author on ascetic virtues; St. Ephrem the Syrian (c. 306–373), the "Harp of the Spirit" (*бг҃одх҃нове́нный*); and St. Eulogius the Presbyter of Alexandria. Physical Leaf p62 = Printed Page 58.

[^163]: St. Theodore of Therme; St. Ennathas; St. Theodulus; St. Theonas; and St. Theodosius the Cenobiarch (c. 423–529), founder of organized communal monasticism in the Judean wilderness. Physical Leaf p62 = Printed Page 58.

[^164]: St. Theoctistus of Palestine (d. 467), co-laborer with St. Euthymius in the Judean desert; and St. Theodore the Sykeote (d. 613), Bishop of Anastasiopolis in Galatia, famed for his austere fasting from childhood and miraculous healings. Physical Leaf p62 = Printed Page 58.

[^165]: St. John Climacus (c. 579–649), Abbot of Saint Catherine's Monastery on Mount Sinai and author of *The Ladder of Divine Ascent* (*Лѣ́ствица / Κλῖмаξ*); and St. Chariton the Confessor (d. c. 350), founder of the Judean lavras of Pharan, Douka, and Souka (the Old Lavra). Physical Leaf p62 = Printed Page 58.

[^166]: St. Euphrasia the Virgin (c. 380–410), daughter of a senator of Constantinople who gave away her immense wealth and embraced strict asceticism in a convent in the Egyptian Thebaid, commemorated alongside the greatest male monastic founders. Physical Leaf p63 = Printed Page 59.

[^167]: An alphabetical acrostic hymnography honoring venerable fathers: St. Hesychius of Jerusalem, Hierax, and Joseph; Callistus, Laurence, and Longinus; St. Leucius the Confessor, St. Maximus the Confessor (c. 580–662), Marcian, and St. Mark the Ascetic (5th c.). Physical Leaf p63 = Printed Page 59.

[^168]: St. Macarius the Great (c. 300–391) and St. Macarius of Alexandria (d. c. 395); Jovian; Mark; and St. Moses the Ethiopian (c. 330–405), former brigand turned desert father and martyr at Scetis. Physical Leaf p64 = Printed Page 60.

[^169]: St. Martinian of Caesarea (4th c.), hermit who endured severe temptations to preserve chastity; St. Marcellus, Abbot of the Monastery of the "Unsleeping Ones" (Akoimetoi) in Constantinople; and St. Miles (Milas), Persian bishop and wonderworker who raised the dead. Physical Leaf p64 = Printed Page 60.

[^170]: St. Nilus of Ancyra (Nilus of Sinai, d. c. 430), disciple of St. John Chrysostom and prolific ascetic epistolographer whose verbal streams water the rational soul; St. Naucratis, brother of St. Basil the Great; St. Nicon; St. Nathanael; and St. Nistheron of Nitria. Physical Leaf p64 = Printed Page 60.

[^171]: St. Xenophon of Constantinople (5th c.), who with his wife Maria and sons Arcadius and John embraced monasticism; St. Porphyrius, Bishop of Gaza (c. 347–420); and St. Onuphrius the Great (4th c.), who lived in complete solitude in the inner Egyptian desert for sixty years. Physical Leaf p64 = Printed Page 60.

[^172]: St. Pambo of Nitria (d. c. 375); St. Paphnutius the Ascetic; Abba Pior; Abba Patermuthius; St. Paul the Simple (4th c.), disciple of St. Anthony famed for absolute obedience and casting out powerful demons; and St. Pitirion, disciple of Anthony on the Mountain of Pispir. Physical Leaf p64 = Printed Page 60.

[^173]: St. Pachomius the Great and his spiritual father Abba Palamon the Hermit; St. Petronius (Petronios), successor of Pachomius; and St. Patapius of Thebes (4th c.), desert hermit who later worked miracles in Constantinople. Physical Leaf p64 = Printed Page 60.

[^174]: St. Rabbula of Edessa (d. 435); St. Rufus; St. Sisoës the Great (d. c. 429), desert father of Scetis who lived an angelic life on the mountain of Anthony; Eradius; and St. Silvanus of Gaza (4th c.). Physical Leaf p65 = Printed Page 61.

[^175]: The unique Byzantine commemoration of the four celebrated ascetic saints bearing the name Simeon: three renowned pillar-saints (*stylites*) — St. Simeon Stylites the Elder (c. 390–459), St. Simeon Stylites the Younger of the Marvelous Mountain (521–596), and St. Simeon Stylites III; and one Holy Fool (*salos* / *ю҆ро́дивый*) — St. Simeon of Emesa (6th c.). Physical Leaf p65 = Printed Page 61.

[^176]: St. Sabbas the Sanctified (439–532), founder of the Great Lavra (Mar Saba) in the Kidron Valley and father of the Palestinian monastic Typikon; St. Serapion the Sindonite or Serapion of Thmuis; and St. Silvanus. Physical Leaf p65 = Printed Page 61.

[^177]: Celebrated Desert Fathers of Egypt: Sarmatas (disciple of Anthony), Timothy, Tithes, Hyperechius, Pharmuthius, Phocas, Chariton, Chaeremon of Nitria, Psoes, and Hor of the Thebaid. Physical Leaf p65 = Printed Page 61.

[^178]: The choir of holy venerable women (*прпⷣбныѧ же́ны*): St. Bryaene, St. Febronia of Nisibis (martyred c. 304), St. Thomaïs, Heria, Platonis, and St. Melania the Younger of Rome (c. 383–439), who established monasteries on the Mount of Olives in Jerusalem. Physical Leaf p66 = Printed Page 62.

[^179]: St. Euphrasia of Tabennisi; the two venerable Theodoras: St. Theodora of Alexandria (5th c., who lived in repentance disguised as a monk named Theodore) and St. Theodora of Thessalonica (9th c.); and the two Anastasias: St. Anastasia the Patrician (6th c., who fled Justinian's court to live in the desert as the monk Anastasius) and St. Anastasia of Rome. Physical Leaf p66 = Printed Page 62.

[^180]: St. Mary of Egypt (c. 344–421), supreme archetype of Lenten repentance who lived 47 years beyond the Jordan; St. Marina, who was brought up in a monastery disguised as a monk under the name Marinus; and St. Euphrosyne of Alexandria (5th c.), who lived in a men's monastery as the monk Smaragdus. Physical Leaf p66 = Printed Page 62.

[^181]: St. Theodula of Anazarbus (martyred under Diocletian); St. Theodote; St. Isaura; and St. Isidora the Fool-for-Christ of Tabennisi (4th c.), who wore a dishcloth on her head and endured contempt in silence until discovered by St. Pitirion. Physical Leaf p66 = Printed Page 62.

[^182]: St. Marina of Antioch; St. Matrona of Perge (5th c.), who founded a convent in Constantinople after living disguised in a men's monastery; St. Syncletica of Alexandria (c. 270–350), desert amma whose teachings are preserved in the *Apophthegmata*; Amma Sarah of Scetis; and St. Justina the Virgin Martyr (d. 304). Physical Leaf p66 = Printed Page 62.

[^183]: St. Pelagia of Antioch (d. c. 457), former actress and courtesan who repented at the preaching of Bishop Nonnus and lived as a recluse on the Mount of Olives; and St. Thaïs of Egypt (4th c.), converted by St. Paphnutius and confined to a cell of penance. Physical Leaf p66 = Printed Page 62.

[^184]: The three supreme Cappadocian and Alexandrian doctors of the Church: St. Basil the Great (c. 330–379), Archbishop of Caesarea; St. Athanasius the Great (c. 296–373), Patriarch of Alexandria and pillar of Nicaea; and St. Gregory the Theologian (Nazianzen, c. 329–390), Archbishop of Constantinople. Physical Leaf p66 = Printed Page 62.

[^185]: St. John Chrysostom (c. 349–407), Archbishop of Constantinople; St. Cyril of Alexandria (d. 444) and St. Cyril of Jerusalem (c. 313–386); St. Hesychius of Jerusalem (5th c.); and St. Meletius, Patriarch of Antioch (d. 381), who presided over the Second Ecumenical Council. Physical Leaf p66 = Printed Page 62.

[^186]: St. Gregory of Nyssa (c. 335–c. 395), mystical theologian and brother of St. Basil; the two wonderworking fathers (St. Gregory the Wonderworker of Neocaesarea and St. Nicholas of Myra); St. Epiphanius of Salamis (c. 315–403), Bishop of Cyprus; and St. Amphilochius, Bishop of Iconium (d. c. 394). Physical Leaf p66 = Printed Page 62.

[^187]: The holy Patriarchs and Bishops of Constantinople and adjacent sees: St. Metrophanes (d. 326), first Bishop of Constantinople; St. Nectarius (d. 397); St. Atticus (d. 425); St. Gennadius (d. 471); St. Anatolius (d. 458); Eusebius; and St. Proclus of Constantinople (d. 446), disciple of Chrysostom. Physical Leaf p66 = Printed Page 62.

[^188]: St. Nicholas of Myra in Lycia (c. 270–343); St. Sophronius, Patriarch of Jerusalem (c. 560–638), renowned hymnographer; Eulalius; St. Diadochus of Photike (5th c.), author on spiritual discernment and the Jesus Prayer; St. Eustathius of Antioch; and Euphecalius. Physical Leaf p66 = Printed Page 62.

[^189]: St. Ambrose, Bishop of Milan (c. 339–397), famed for fearless pastoral boldness before Emperor Theodosius; St. Hierotheus, first Bishop of Athens and disciple of St. Paul; and the holy Bishops named Alexander (St. Alexander of Alexandria, opponent of Arius, and St. Alexander of Constantinople). Physical Leaf p67 = Printed Page 63.

[^190]: Divine teachers and ascetics: St. Felix, Pope of Rome; St. Spyridon the Wonderworker, Bishop of Trimythous (c. 270–348); Antipater of Bostra (5th c.); Abba Pambo; Palladius, Bishop of Helenopolis (c. 363–c. 430), author of the *Lausiac History*; Nonnus, Bishop of Edessa; St. Jerome of Stridon (c. 347–420), translator of the Vulgate; and St. Germanus, Patriarch of Constantinople (c. 634–733). Physical Leaf p67 = Printed Page 63.

[^191]: St. Dionysius the Areopagite, disciple of St. Paul and mystical theologian; St. Clement, Pope of Rome and hieromartyr (d. c. 99); St. Flavian, Patriarch of Constantinople and confessor (d. 449); and St. Paul the Confessor, Patriarch of Constantinople (d. c. 350). Physical Leaf p67 = Printed Page 63.

[^192]: Champion defenders of the holy icons during the Iconoclast controversies: St. Michael the Confessor, Bishop of Synnada (d. 826); St. Tarasius, Patriarch of Constantinople (d. 806), who presided over the Seventh Ecumenical Council (Nicaea II, 787); St. Nicephorus the Confessor, Patriarch of Constantinople (758–828); St. Theodore the Studite (759–826); and St. Theophanes the Chronicler ("Theophanes of sacred name", c. 759–818). Physical Leaf p67 = Printed Page 63.

[^193]: Apostolic hieromartyrs: St. Peter, chief of the Apostles; St. Ignatius the God-bearer, Bishop of Antioch (d. c. 108); St. Polycarp, Bishop of Smyrna (c. 69–155); and St. Cyprian, Bishop of Carthage and martyr (d. 258). Physical Leaf p67 = Printed Page 63.

[^194]: 1 Corinthians 2:9 / Isaiah 64:4 ("Eye hath not seen, nor ear heard, neither have entered into the heart of man, the things which God hath prepared for them that love Him"). The ascetic fathers counted all transient worldly glory as dung (Philippians 3:8) and spiderwebs in comparison to the eternal kingdom of Christ. Physical Leaf p68 = Printed Page 64.

[^195]: Psalm 18:5 LXX (Psalm 19:4 MT) / Romans 10:18 ("Their sound went into all the earth, and their words unto the ends of the world"), applied liturgically to the worldwide moral and spiritual victory of the holy ascetic fathers over the demons. Physical Leaf p68 = Printed Page 64.

[^196]: Genesis 2:7 (the fashioning of Adam from the dust of the earth and the inbreathing of the breath of life) and Genesis 3:1–6 (the deception of humanity by Satan through the serpent and the forbidden fruit); setting the theological theme of Cheesefare Sunday: the tragedy of the Fall and the expulsion from Paradise. Physical Leaf p69 = Printed Page 65.

[^197]: Genesis 3:7 (Adam and Eve sewing fig leaves together to make aprons), Genesis 3:21 (the Lord God fashioning coats of skins for Adam and his wife), and Genesis 3:17–19 (the curse upon the ground, bringing forth thorns and thistles, and eating bread by the sweat of the brow). Christ the New Adam clothes fallen humanity once more in the God-woven robe of baptismal grace. Physical Leaf p69 = Printed Page 65.

[^198]: Genesis 3:23–24. The expulsion of Adam from the Garden of Eden and the placing of the Cherubim with a flaming sword to bar access unto the Tree of Life. In this celebrated idiomelon, Adam apostrophizes Paradise itself, begging the rustling of its leaves to intercede with the Creator. Physical Leaf p69 = Printed Page 65.

[^199]: Joel 2:10; 2:31; Matthew 27:45. The cosmic mourning over the Fall of humanity and the locking of the gates of Paradise: creation trembles, the sun hides its light, and the heavenly bodies are obscured as Adam departs into exile weeping and striking his face. Physical Leaf p70 = Printed Page 66.

[^200]: 1 Timothy 2:4 ("Who will have all men to be saved, and to come unto the knowledge of the truth") and John 6:37 ("him that comes to Me I will in no wise cast out"). In the midst of Adam's bitter lamentation outside the closed gates of Eden, the Savior responds with the consoling promise of the Incarnation and universal redemption through repentance. Physical Leaf p70 = Printed Page 66.

[^201]: Cross-reference to fol. 20 (Physical Leaf p24), where the penitential troparion *Мно́жества содѣ́ѧнныхъ мно́ю лю́тыхъ* (*"When I consider the multitude of evil deeds done by me"*) is printed in full following Psalm 50 at Sunday Matins during the pre-Lenten and Lenten seasons. Physical Leaf p70 = Printed Page 66.

[^202]: St. Christopher Protosynkellos (9th c.), hymnographer of the Great Church of Hagia Sophia in Constantinople and author of the Cheesefare Sunday Matins Canon, meditating upon the cosmic catastrophe of Adam's expulsion from Eden and summoning the soul to lament its passions before the dawn of the Great Fast. Physical Leaf p70 = Printed Page 66.

[^203]: The Heirmos of Ode 1 from the Cheesefare Sunday Matins Canon by St. Christopher Protosynkellos (*Я́кѡ по су́ху...* / Exodus 14:21–31), sung as the Katavasia. Physical Leaf p71 = Printed Page 67.

[^204]: Genesis 2:16–17 and 3:1–6. The deception of Eve by the serpent, the eating of the fruit of the tree of knowledge (*дре́во разꙋ́мное* / *lignum scientiae boni et mali*), and the immediate loss of original righteousness and divine glory. Physical Leaf p71 = Printed Page 67.

[^205]: Genesis 3:17–19 and 3:23–24. The divine judgment upon Adam: condemned to till the ground whence he was taken and to eat bread in the sweat of his face. The liturgical hymnody presents this sorrow as the archetypal spiritual justification for Christian ascetic fasting and bodily temperance (*воздержа́нїе*). Physical Leaf p71 = Printed Page 67.

[^206]: The patristic Matins homily prescribed following the Sessional Hymns: St. Gregory the Theologian (Nazianzen, c. 329–390), *Oration 16: On His Father's Silence, Because of the Plague of Hail* (*Εἰς τὸν πατέρα σιωπῶντα διὰ τὴν πληγὴν τῆς χαλάζης* / *Въ ꙗ҆́звѣ гра́да*), opening with the words: *Что̀ рче́мъ ча́инѣ похвалѧ́емый;* (*Quid dicemus...?*). The rubric prescribes two intervals (*далї́ла два̀* / Greek *διαλείμματα*) during the monastic reading. Physical Leaf p72 = Printed Page 68.

[^207]: The cosmic and pastoral grief of fallen Adam: apostrophizing the heavenly hosts, the meadows, and the trees of Paradise to weep for him, shedding their foliage like tears (*ѿ ли́ствѡвъ ꙗ҆́коже ѿ ѻ҆че́съ*) over his stripped nakedness and loss of the divine likeness. Physical Leaf p72 = Printed Page 68.

[^208]: Genesis 2:7, 3:19. The dust of the earth from which Adam was formed, contrasted with his fall into death and hades through disobedience, and the prayer to the Virgin Mother of God as the mystical bridal chamber (*черто́гъ сла́вы та́йный*) who leads humanity back to the heavenly bridal chamber. Physical Leaf p72 = Printed Page 68.

[^209]: The celebrated Kontakion (Tone 6) and Ikos of Cheesefare Sunday, composed by St. Romanos the Melodist (c. 490–c. 556). Adam sits opposite the garden of Eden weeping and striking his brow, imploring the sound of Paradise's rustling leaves (*шꙋ́момъ твои́хъ ли́ствїй*) to intercede before the Creator that the gates be not forever closed against him. Physical Leaf p73 = Printed Page 69.

[^210]: The historical and theological Synaxarion of Cheesefare Sunday (*Воспомина́нїе ѿ ра́йскїѧ пи́щи и҆спаде́нїѧ первозда́ннагѡ А҆да́ма*), expounding the ascetic rationale of the Great Fast as the therapeutic antidote (*по́стнаѧ лѣ́чба*) to Adam's intemperance. It correlates Adam's transgression on the sixth day at the sixth hour with Christ the New Adam stretching forth His hands upon the Cross at the sixth hour on Great Friday to heal the ancient ruin. Physical Leaf p73 = Printed Page 69.

[^211]: Patristic allegorical interpretations of the trees in Eden: St. Gregory the Theologian interpreting the tree of knowledge as divine contemplation (*мы́сль бж҃е́ственнаѧ*) reserved for the spiritually mature; St. John Chrysostom teaching that Paradise was both sensible and intelligible (*ᲂу҆́мный и҆ чꙋ́вственный*); and the ancient tradition identifying the tree of disobedience with the fig tree (*смоко́вница*), prefiguring Christ's cursing of the barren fig tree (Matt. 21:19). Physical Leaf p73 = Printed Page 69.

[^212]: The architectural structure of the Lenten Triodion (*всю̀ Трїѡ́дь*): moving from Old Testament typologies (Creation, the Fall, Moses, the Prophets, the Psalms) to the New Testament economy (the Annunciation permanently anchored within Lent, Lazarus Saturday, Palm Sunday, Great and Holy Week, the Saving Passion, the Resurrection, and the reading of the Acts of the Apostles through Pentecost). Physical Leaf p74 = Printed Page 70.

[^213]: The ancient Patristic and canonical doctrine of the Great Forty Days as the tithe of the whole year (*десѧти́на є҆́сть всего̀ лѣ́та*): offering one-tenth of our days to God in repentance, fasting, and compunction, alongside the three other seasonal fasts of the Church year (Apostles' Fast, Dormition Fast, Nativity Fast). Physical Leaf p74 = Printed Page 70.

[^214]: Daniel 3:49–50 LXX. The Angel of the Lord making the interior of the Babylonian furnace like a cool whistling wind of dew (*росода́тельнꙋ ᲂу҆́бѡ пе́щь*), saving the Three Holy Youths (Ananias, Azarias, and Misael), functioning as the heirmological model for Ode 7. Physical Leaf p74 = Printed Page 70.

[^215]: 1 Kings 18:38 LXX (1 Kings 18:38 MT). Elijah's sacrifice upon Mount Carmel consumed by fire from heaven despite being drenched with water, demonstrating the omnipotence of God Who accomplishes all things by His mere will; the heirmological Katavasia of Ode 8. Physical Leaf p75 = Printed Page 71.

[^216]: Genesis 3:24. The Cherubim placed at the east of the garden of Eden with a flaming sword turning every way (*пла́менное ѻ҆рꙋ́жїе*) to guard the way to the Tree of Life, barring fallen humanity until Christ reopened Paradise on the Cross to the Repentant Thief. Physical Leaf p75 = Printed Page 71.

[^217]: The Praises (*На хвали́техъ*) idiomelon in Tone 5 (*Оу҆вы̀ мнѣ̀, А҆да́мъ рыда́нїемъ возопѝ...*), lamenting the loss of humanity's primordial royal authority over earthly creation and the donning of the mortal garment of skins (*ᲂу҆мерщвле́нїѧ ко́жꙋ* / Genesis 3:21). Physical Leaf p75 = Printed Page 71.

[^218]: Ephesians 6:11–17. The classical Lenten idiomelon *По́прище добродѣ́телей ѿве́рзесѧ* (*"The arena of virtues hath opened"*), depicting the Great Fast as an ascetical stadium where combatants arm themselves with the panoply of the Cross: faith as an unshakeable wall, prayer as a shield, almsgiving as a helmet, and fasting as a sword cleaving evil from the heart. Physical Leaf p76 = Printed Page 72.

[^219]: Exodus 24:18, 34:28. Moses fasting forty days and forty nights on Mount Sinai, cleansing his spiritual vision to become a God-beholder (*бг҃ови́децъ*), serving as the archetype for the Christian's forty-day ascent to Pascha. Physical Leaf p76 = Printed Page 72.

[^220]: Epistle and Gospel pericopes for Cheesefare Sunday Liturgy: Romans 13:11–14:4 (pericope 112: "The night is far spent, the day is at hand... let us cast off the works of darkness") and Matthew 6:14–21 (pericope 17: fasting, forgiveness of debts, and laying up treasure in heaven). Physical Leaf p76 = Printed Page 72.

[^221]: The liturgical-rubrical statute governing the Polyeleos (*Зрѝ: Подоба́етъ вѣ́дати...*): the festive chanting of Psalms 134–135 is suspended from Cheesefare Sunday until the Exaltation of the Precious Cross (September 14), sung only on Great Master feasts with Vigil and major ranked feasts of saints. Physical Leaf p76 = Printed Page 72.

[^222]: The solemn Vespers of Cheesefare Sunday (popularly called Forgiveness Vespers), marking the formal rubrical entrance into the Great Fast. The stichera at *Lord, I have cried* are distributed among the Octoechos penitential stichera (Tone of the week), the Triodion stichera of St. Joseph the Hymnographer and St. Theodore the Studite, and the Menaion. Physical Leaf p77 = Printed Page 73.

[^223]: Jonah 3:5–10. The Ninevites repenting in sackcloth and fasting at the preaching of Jonah, invoked in St. Joseph the Hymnographer's sticheron (*...ꙗ҆́коже дре́вле Нїневї́таны*) as a paradigm of divine mercy toward sinners. Physical Leaf p77 = Printed Page 73.

[^224]: Psalm 68:17 LXX (Psalm 69:16 MT). The Great Prokeimenon in Tone 8: *Не ѿвратѝ лица̀ твоегѡ̀ ѿ ѻ҆́трока твоегѡ̀...* (*"Turn not away Thy face from Thy servant, for I am afflicted; hear me speedily: draw near unto my soul, and redeem it"*), chanted with four solemn verses, accompanied by the rubrical change of clerical vestments and altar coverings from light to dark penitential hue. Physical Leaf p77 = Printed Page 73.

[^225]: Romans 13:12. The Vespers Aposticha idiomelon in Tone 4 (*Возсїѧ̀ бл҃года́ть твоѧ̀ Гдⷭ҇и...*): announcing that the acceptable time and season of repentance has dawned, summoning the faithful to cast off the works of darkness and put on the armor of light. Physical Leaf p78 = Printed Page 74.

[^226]: The four solemn Lenten troparia chanted with metanias/prostrations at the end of Lenten Vespers: *Бцⷣе Дв҃о ра́дꙋйсѧ* (Prostration 1), *Крⷭ҇ти́телю Хрⷭ҇то́въ* (Prostration 2), *Моли́те ѡ҆ на́съ ст҃і́и а҆пⷭ҇ли* (Prostration 3), and *Подъ твою̀ ми́лость* (bow without prostration). Physical Leaf p78 = Printed Page 74.

[^227]: The venerable Prayer of St. Ephrem the Syrian (*Гдⷭ҇и и҆ Влⷣко живота̀ моегѡ̀...*), recited with three great prostrations, followed by the mutual Rite of Forgiveness (*цѣлꙋемъ ст҃ы̑ѧ и҆ честны̑ѧ і҆кѡ́ны*): the clergy and brethren prostrate before one another asking forgiveness for all sins committed during the past year. Physical Leaf p78 = Printed Page 74.

[^228]: The rubrics for Small Compline on Cheesefare Sunday evening and throughout the weeks of Great Lent: penitential prostrations, recitation of the Prayer of St. Ephrem, and the omission of the Canon to the Mother of God unless a Vigil is kept. Physical Leaf p79 = Printed Page 75.

[^229]: The special memorial legislation (*Подоба́етъ вѣ́дати...*) for a monastic brother who departs unto the Lord during the weekdays of Great Lent: weekday funeral pannikhidas and Liturgies are forbidden; the third-day remembrance is deferred to Friday evening and Saturday Liturgy, the ninth-day remembrance to the following Saturday, and the fortieth-day remembrance begins from New Sunday (St. Thomas Sunday). Physical Leaf p79 = Printed Page 75.

[^230]: Physical Folio 76 (Page_0080.jpg) marks the primary codicological division of the codex: the formal beginning of the Holy and Great Forty Days (*Нача́ло ст҃а́гѡ и҆ вели́кагѡ поста̀*). Physical Leaf p80 = Printed Page 76.

[^231]: The awakening bell on Clean Monday: the paraecclesiarch sounds the klepalo before dawn (*зна́менаетъ... къ свѣ́тꙋ*), allowing extra rest on account of the evening collation (*ко́нѣ ра́ди вече́рнягѡ ᲂу҆тѣше́нїа*). Physical Leaf p80 = Printed Page 76.

[^232]: The monastic discipline of the Six Psalms (*Шестоѱа́лмїе*): Psalms 3, 37, 62, 87, 102, and 142 LXX, during which absolute silence, immobility, and contrition are mandated: no whispering, spitting, or throat-clearing, with arms folded upon the breast and heads bowed. Physical Leaf p80 = Printed Page 76.

[^233]: Isaiah 26:9, 11, 15 LXX. The four solemn verses chanted with Alleluia in the Tone of the Octoechos (*Ѿ но́щи ᲂу҆́тренюетъ дꙋ́хъ мо́й...*) at Lenten Matins, replacing "God is the Lord." Physical Leaf p80 = Printed Page 76.

[^234]: The Triadica (*Трⷪ҇чны*) chanted with slow, compunctious melody (*велегла́снѡ и҆ ко́снѡ съ пѣ́нїемъ*), followed by the first stichology of the Psalter with three prostrations after each antiphon kathisma. Physical Leaf p80 = Printed Page 76.

[^235]: Reading from Saint Ephrem the Syrian, which traditionally begins on Clean Monday and continues through Great Lent until Palm Sunday.

[^236]: Sessional Hymn of the Triodion by Lord Joseph the Hymnographer, Tone 2, Model: "Being the fountain of tender mercy".

[^237]: Reading from the Lausiac History (*Lavsaik*) of Palladius of Helenopolis, a monastic collection read during Lenten Matins.

[^238]: Rubrics for singing the Biblical Odes (*Господеви поемъ* / *Let us sing unto the Lord*, Exod. 15:1) with the Canon of the Menaion and the Triodia of Joseph and Theodore the Studite.

[^239]: Rubrical prescription concerning the omission of the Octoechos on weekdays throughout the Holy Forty Days (except on Sundays), singing only the Menaion and the Triodia of Joseph and Theodore the Studite.

[^240]: Canon and Triodion by Lord Joseph the Hymnographer, Ode 1, Tone 2, Heirmos: "Come, ye peoples".

[^241]: Triodion, Ode 8, Heirmos: "Into the fiery furnace".

[^242]: Another Triodion, Ode 8, by Theodore the Studite, Heirmos: "Of old Who dewed the Hebrew youths in the flame".

[^243]: The Magnificat rubric (*И стихословится Честнѣйшая*) directing the chanting of the Canticle of the Theotokos ("My soul magnifies the Lord") with the refrain "More honorable than the Cherubim".

[^244]: Triodion, Ode 9, Heirmos: "God the Word from God".

[^245]: Another Triodion, Ode 9, by Theodore the Studite, Heirmos: "Her who supernaturally conceived in her womb".

[^246]: Lenten rubrics for Lauds (*Хвалитны* / *The Praises*): on ordinary Lenten weekdays, stichera are omitted unless the Menaion provides stichera for a second saint, and the three Laudate psalms (Pss. 148, 149, 150) are recited smoothly without chanting.

[^247]: Lenten Matins Aposticha self-governing sticheron (*самогласенъ*), Tone 5: "The Fast has arrived, mother of chastity".

[^248]: Order of the 16 prostrations (metanias) with the Prayer of St. Ephrem the Syrian (*Господи и Владыко живота моего*): three great prostrations, twelve small metanias with "O God, cleanse me a sinner," and one final great prostration reciting the entire prayer.

[^249]: Order of the First Hour in Great Lent: no kathisma on Mondays (or on Fridays at the First and Ninth Hours), with the Lenten troparion of the First Hour sung in Tone 6 ("In the morning give ear unto my voice") with three prostrations to the ground.

[^250]: Rubric on the reading of the Catecheses of St. Theodore the Studite in the narthex on Wednesdays and Fridays, and the rule of monastic silence after dismissal.

[^251]: The Third Hour order in Great Lent: striking the wooden klepalo (*било*), ordinary kathisma, Lenten troparion of the Third Hour ("O Lord, Who didst send down Thy Most Holy Spirit at the third hour upon Thine Apostles"), and reading from the *Ladder of Divine Ascent* of St. John Climacus (*Лествичникъ*).

[^252]: The monitor monk appointed throughout Great Lent to quietly circulate through the church, observe anyone nodding off, and gently rouse them to hear the spiritual readings.

[^253]: The Sixth Hour order in Great Lent: striking the klepalo six times, reciting the kathisma, and chanting the Lenten troparion of the Sixth Hour in Tone 2 ("Who on the sixth day and hour didst nail to the Cross").

[^254]: Troparion of the Prophecy, Tone 5: "O Lord, O Lord, at Whom all things do tremble".

[^255]: Prophecy Pericope from Isaiah (Isa. 1:1–20), appointed for the Sixth Hour of Clean Monday, denouncing hollow ritualism and demanding repentance, justice, and purity.

[^256]: Prokeimenon following the Prophecy, Tone 7, Psalm 2: "Serve the Lord with fear, and rejoice unto Him with trembling" (Ps. 2:11).

[^257]: Typikon rubric on the two customs regarding the Ninth Hour: whether the brethren disperse to cells after the Sixth Hour (striking the klepalo nine times to reassemble), or whether the Ninth Hour follows immediately upon the prayer of St. Basil the Great ("O God and Lord of hosts").

[^258]: The Ninth Hour Lenten troparion, Tone 8: "Who at the ninth hour for our sake didst taste death in the flesh," sung with three prostrations to the ground.

[^259]: The Typika (Beatitudes) chanted solemnly and slowly in Tone 8 with the refrain: "Remember us, O Lord, when Thou comest into Thy kingdom," followed by three great prostrations at "Remember us, O Lord... Remember us, O Master... Remember us, O Holy One...".

[^260]: The Creed (*Вѣрую*) recited during the Typika, followed by the prayer of remission (*Ослаби, остави*), the Lord's Prayer, and the appointed kontakia.

[^261]: The 16 Lenten prostrations concluding the Typika (*изобразительныя поклоны*), following which the paraecclesiarch receives the superior's blessing to ring the bell for Lenten Vespers.

[^262]: The opening of Lenten Vespers: Psalm 103 (*Bless the Lord, O my soul*) read quietly by the reader while the priest recites the Prayers of Lamplighting before the holy doors, followed by the 18th Kathisma (Pss. 119–133, *Ко Господу внегда скорбѣти ми*).

[^263]: Vespers on Monday evening of Clean Week (*Въ понедѣльникъ первыя седмицы святаго Великаго Поста вечеръ*), where at "Lord, I have cried" 6 stichera are appointed: 3 from the Triodion (by Joseph and Theodore the Studite) and 3 from the Menaion.

[^264]: Stichera at "Lord, I have cried" by Lord Joseph the Hymnographer, Tone 2, Model: "All things have passed away".

[^265]: Another sticheron by Lord Theodore the Studite, Tone 5, Model: "O venerable Father".

[^266]: Prokeimenon at Lenten Vespers, Tone 6, Psalm 3: "Salvation is of the Lord, and Thy blessing is upon Thy people" (Ps. 3:9), Verse: "Lord, why are they multiplied that afflict me?" (Ps. 3:2).

[^267]: The first reading from Genesis (Gen. 1:1–13), appointed for the Vespers of Clean Monday, recounting the creation of the heavens and earth, light, the firmament, the dry land, and plant life during the first three days of creation.

[^268]: Second Prokeimenon at Vespers of Clean Monday, Tone 5, Psalm 4: "The Lord will hear me when I cry unto Him" (Ps. 4:4), Verse: "When I called, the God of my righteousness heard me" (Ps. 4:2).

[^269]: The reading from Proverbs (Prov. 1:1–20), appointed for the Vespers of Clean Monday, establishing the fear of the Lord as the foundation of wisdom and warning the young against the destructive counsels and robbery of sinners.

[^270]: Lenten Vespers Aposticha self-governing sticheron (*самогласенъ* / *idiomelon*), Tone 3: "Let us fast an acceptable fast, well-pleasing unto the Lord".

[^271]: Lenten Vespers dismissal rite: chanting the penitential troparia with three prostrations ("O Virgin Mother of God, rejoice", "O Baptist of Christ", "Pray for us"), 40 repetitions of "Lord, have mercy", and the Prayer of St. Ephrem the Syrian with 16 prostrations.

[^272]: Post-Vespers rubrics: recitation of Psalm 144 (*I will extol Thee, my God*) before the refectory meal, followed by chanting the patronal sticheron and the memorial litya for departed brethren in the narthex.

[^273]: Typikon rubric concerning the omission of the Divine Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts on Clean Monday and Clean Tuesday, and the strict monastic fast according to the Athonite Typikon requiring total abstinence from food on the first day of Great Lent.

[^274]: Dietary rule for Tuesday, Wednesday, and Thursday of Clean Week: one litra (approx. 12 ounces) of dry bread with water, and if desired, salt with *ukrop* (a warm infusion of water with dill, fennel, or other herbs permitted on dry-fasting days).

[^275]: Rubrical distinction between the Lavra of St. Sabbas (where monks recited Great Compline individually in their cells) and Palestinian cenobitic monasteries (where Great Compline was chanted communally in church).

[^276]: Clean Monday Great Compline (*на повече́рїи вели́комъ*): summoned by 12 strokes on the wooden klepalo at the tenth hour (approx. 4:00 PM), opening with Psalm 69 before the Great Canon.

[^277]: Division of the Great Penitential Canon of St. Andrew of Crete into four sections, chanted at Great Compline across the first four days of Clean Week (Monday through Thursday), prior to its complete chanting at Matins of Thursday of the Fifth Week.

[^278]: St. Andrew of Crete (c. 660–740), born in Damascus, monk of the Brotherhood of the Holy Sepulchre in Jerusalem, deacon of the Great Church in Constantinople, and Archbishop of Gortyna in Crete, hymnographer and creator of the Great Canon.

[^279]: Great Canon, Ode 2, Tone 6, Heirmos: "Give ear, O heaven, and I will speak" (Deut. 32:1), focusing on Adam's fall, the loss of the original robe of light, and the soul's voluntary submission to the passions.

[^280]: Great Canon, Ode 3, Tone 6, Heirmos: "Upon the immovable rock of Thy commandments", with troparia warning the soul to flee the conflagration of Sodom unto Zoar, and petitioning Christ the Good Shepherd to seek His strayed sheep.

[^281]: Great Canon, Ode 4, Tone 6, Heirmos: "The Prophet heard of Thy coming, O Lord, and was afraid" (Hab. 3:2), reflecting on the fleeting nature of earthly life, Jacob's ladder of divine ascent, and the spiritual allegories of Leah (ascetic practice / *praxis*) and Rachel (spiritual contemplation / *theoria*).

[^282]: Great Canon, Ode 5, Tone 6, Heirmos: "Early out of the night waking early unto Thee, O Lover of mankind" (Isa. 26:9), reflecting on Reuben's incestuous transgressions and Joseph's betrayal by his brothers as types of betrayal and of the Lord's burial and resurrection.

[^283]: Great Canon, Ode 6, Tone 6, Heirmos: "I cried with my whole heart unto the merciful God" (Jonah 2:3), recounting the rebellion of Dathan and Abiram, the backsliding heifer Ephraim (Hos. 4:16), and Moses' hand cleansed of leprosy (Exod. 4:6–7).

[^284]: Kontakion of the Great Canon, Tone 6, attributed to St. Romanos the Melodist: "My soul, my soul, arise! Why sleepest thou?", calling the soul to spiritual alertness before the imminent coming of Christ the Judge.

[^285]: Great Canon, Ode 7, Tone 6, Heirmos: "We have sinned, we have transgressed" (Dan. 9:5), with troparia meditating on Saul seeking his father's asses and unexpectedly receiving a kingdom, and David's double sin (adultery and murder) followed by swift, exemplary repentance in Psalm 50.

[^286]: Great Canon, Ode 8, Tone 6, Heirmos: "Him Whom the heavenly hosts glorify" (Dan. 3:57), recalling the fiery ascent of Elijah the charioteer, Elisha receiving a double portion of the spirit and parting the Jordan, the hospitality of the Shunammite woman, and the greed and leprosy of Gehazi.

[^287]: Great Canon, Ode 9, Tone 6, Heirmos: "Ineffable is the childbearing of a seedless conception", transitioning from the figures of the Old Witness to the saving economy of Christ in the New Testament: the salvation of the Magi, the Slaughter of the Holy Innocents, and the Lord's forty-day fast in the wilderness.

[^288]: Concluding troparion of the Great Canon addressed directly to the author, St. Andrew of Crete: "O venerable Andrew, thrice-blessed father, shepherd of Crete, cease not to pray for those that hymn thee".

[^289]: Order of the second stasis of Great Compline: Psalms 4, 6, 12, followed by the second stasis (Pss. 24, 30, 90), the solemn chanting of "God is with us" (Isa. 8:8–18), the Hymn of the Bodiless Powers, the Nicene Creed, petitionary verses with alternating bows, and the troparia of the First Tone/Second Tone ("Enlighten mine eyes, O Christ God").

[^290]: Alternate Lenten troparia for Great Compline appointed on Tuesday and Thursday evenings, Tone 8: "The unsleeping vigilance of mine invisible enemies" (*Невидимыхъ врагъ моихъ несыпанїе*).

[^291]: Third stasis of Great Compline: Psalms 50, 101, the Penitential Prayer of King Manasseh (2 Chron. 36 / Odes of Scripture), the troparion "Lord of hosts, be with us" (*Господи силъ съ нами буди*) chanted antiphonally with five psalm verses, the Prayer of St. Ephrem with 16 prostrations, and the Compline bedtime prayers ("O undefiled, unstained" and "And grant unto us, O Master").

[^292]: Rubric concerning the prayer of great intercession ("O Master great in mercy, Lord Jesus Christ our God", *Владыко многомилостиве*) recited by the priest while all clergy and brethren remain prostrate upon the earth.

[^293]: The ancient monastic Rite of Mutual Forgiveness (*Чинъ прощенїя*) concluding Great Compline: the superior makes a full prostration before the brotherhood asking pardon, the brethren respond, and all clergy and monastics exchange mutual prostrations and forgiveness.
